Top risks

Critical Known vulnerable or malicious driver on the host
Kernel compromise (BYOVD): an attacker loads this known-vulnerable or malicious driver to kill security tools, read credentials or run code in the kernel.
2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Critical Password left in a Windows setup answer file
Credential theft from files: any user reads the administrator or domain join password left by Windows setup and logs on with it, on this host and on every host built from the same image.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
High Defender exclusion
Defense evasion: an attacker drops tools or payloads in the excluded folder, file type or process, and Defender does not scan them; the exclusion list itself tells an attacker where to hide.
5 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
High User right granted to more principals than the baseline
Privilege escalation: rights such as debug programs, backup, restore, impersonate or act as part of the OS let their holders read credentials or become SYSTEM.
5 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
High SMB server signing not required
NTLM relay to SMB: an attacker who coerces or captures an authentication relays it to this host and runs commands with the victim's rights (ntlmrelayx).
2 finding(s) on 2 host(s) · fix script available
High Local account password probably shared with other hosts
Pass-the-hash across the fleet: the hash of the shared local password, taken from one host, logs on to all the others.
2 finding(s) on 2 host(s)

Domain takeover: Zerologon-style Netlogon abuse (CVE-2020-1472) against an allow-listed or vulnerable channel, or Server Operators scheduling tasks that run as SYSTEM on the domain controller.
3 finding(s) on 1 host(s) · fix script available
High EDR agent installed but not running
Blind spot: the host is not monitored, so an attacker works there without detection or response, often after stopping the agent on purpose.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
High SMB server signing not required
NTLM relay to SMB: an attacker who coerces or captures an authentication relays it to this host and runs commands with the victim's rights (ntlmrelayx).
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s) · fix script available
Medium Domain controller does not require LDAP signing or channel binding
NTLM relay to LDAP: an attacker who coerces or captures an authentication relays it to the domain controller to create accounts, set resource-based delegation or grant rights (ntlmrelayx), up to domain takeover.
2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Medium Windows Server 2019 setting differs from the DISA STIG
Depends on the rule: each DISA STIG setting closes a specific attack path (credential theft, relay, privilege escalation, logging gaps); the rule ID in the finding names it.
2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Medium Domain controller is missing a CIS Windows Server security setting
Weakened domain controller defenses: weak Windows Hello for Business keys (ROCA) accepted for logon, or NTLM use in the domain left unaudited so NTLM relay and downgrade go unnoticed.
2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)

High Windows 11 setting differs from the DISA STIG

3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Medium BitLocker volume uses a weak encryption method
Weaker disk protection: AES-CBC 128 or hardware encryption (self-encrypting drives with known flaws) is easier to attack offline than XTS-AES 256 if the device is stolen.
2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Low BitLocker without pre-boot PIN
Key extraction from a stolen device: with TPM-only unlock the key is released at boot without any secret, so TPM bus sniffing, DMA or cold-boot attacks on the running machine can recover it.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Low Defender exclusion
Defense evasion: an attacker drops tools or payloads in the excluded folder, file type or process, and Defender does not scan them; the exclusion list itself tells an attacker where to hide.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Low Windows support ends soon
Soon no security fixes: once support ends, new Windows vulnerabilities stay exploitable on this host.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Low Remote access tool installed
Persistent remote control: an attacker uses (or installs) a legitimate remote access tool to come back from the internet, past the VPN and the firewall.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)

High Local account password probably shared with other hosts
Pass-the-hash across the fleet: the hash of the shared local password, taken from one host, logs on to all the others.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Medium Windows Server 2022 setting differs from the DISA STIG
Depends on the rule: each DISA STIG setting closes a specific attack path (credential theft, relay, privilege escalation, logging gaps); the rule ID in the finding names it.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Low Built-in Administrator enabled
Password attacks on a known account: the RID-500 Administrator exists on every host, is exempt from lockout by default, and its shared password lets an attacker move from host to host (pass-the-hash).
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Low Extra member of local Administrators
Privilege abuse and lateral movement: every extra administrator is another account whose theft gives full control of the host and its stored credentials.
1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)

Score per host = 100 × e−penalty/100, where penalty sums a weight per triggered check at its worst severity (Critical 20, High 8, Medium 3, Low 1); any Critical caps it at 35, any High at 65. It measures exposure: DISA STIG rules and the Brave policy gaps are compliance, shown on their own, and a KEV name match already judged by version (CVE data) is not counted twice.

CIS, DISA STIG and the ACSC Essential Eight cut across the categories: a finding can count in more than one row. The Essential Eight rows are the HostBadger checks that judge a requirement of each strategy; the maturity levels are in the dashboard.

Findings by check
CriticalKnown vulnerable or malicious driver on the hostvulnerable_driver_present · Privilege Escalation2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The file of a kernel driver installed on the host has the SHA-256 of a driver in the LOLDrivers catalog. A vulnerable driver is signed and loads normally, but its flaws let an administrator, or malware that got admin rights, read and write kernel memory: that is how attackers switch off EDR agents and protections ("bring your own vulnerable driver"). Critical when the driver is a known malicious one, High for a vulnerable driver that is loaded, Medium when it is installed but not running; one level lower when LOLDrivers has not verified the sample. The match is by exact file hash, so a fixed version of the same driver does not match.
Attacks it enables. Kernel compromise (BYOVD): an attacker loads this known-vulnerable or malicious driver to kill security tools, read credentials or run code in the kernel.
Remediation. Find the software that installed the driver; update it to a version with a fixed driver, or uninstall it and delete the driver service. A known malicious driver means the host is compromised: isolate it and investigate. Keep the Microsoft vulnerable driver blocklist on, with HVCI where the hardware allows.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1068
SeverityHostObjectDetail
CriticalWKS-ACCT-017driver: kbdhelperC:\Users\jsmith\AppData\Local\Temp\kbdhelper.sys is a known malicious driver (gmer64.sys, LOLDrivers 7ce8fb06-46eb-4f4f-90d5-5518a6561f15); state Stopped, start Manual.
HighWKS-ACCT-017driver: RTCore64C:\Program Files (x86)\MSI Afterburner\RTCore64.sys is a known vulnerable driver (RTCore64.sys, LOLDrivers e32bc3da-4db1-4858-a62c-6fbe4db6afbd); state Running, start Auto.
CriticalPassword left in a Windows setup answer fileunattend_credentials · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A Windows setup answer file (Unattend.xml, autounattend.xml, sysprep.xml or sysprep.inf in the Panther, Sysprep or root folder) still holds a password: the local administrator, an autologon account or the account that joined the domain. Setup replaces the values it used with *SENSITIVE*DATA*DELETED*; these were not. Base64 in these files is not encryption. Every user who can read the file has the password, and an image built from the same answer file shares it. A domain join account in the file is Critical.
Attacks it enables. Credential theft from files: any user reads the administrator or domain join password left by Windows setup and logs on with it, on this host and on every host built from the same image.
Remediation. Delete the answer file (or remove the password values), change the passwords it held on every host built from it, and keep answer files with credentials out of images: use a provisioning account that is disabled after the build, and LAPS for the local administrator.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1552.001
SeverityHostObjectDetail
CriticalWKS-ACCT-017file: C:\Windows\Panther\Unattend.xml2 password value(s) that setup did not remove, among them the credentials of the account that joins the domain.
HighDefender exclusiondefender_exclusion · Antivirus5 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
Why it matters. Files matching an exclusion are never scanned. A broad or user-writable exclusion (a drive, a profile, Temp, Downloads, a script extension, a scripting host) is where malware is dropped on purpose; it is High. Narrow exclusions for known software are Low, worth an occasional review.
Attacks it enables. Defense evasion: an attacker drops tools or payloads in the excluded folder, file type or process, and Defender does not scan them; the exclusion list itself tells an attacker where to hide.
Remediation. Remove broad exclusions; scope the rest to the exact file or folder of the product that needs it, ideally under Program Files where users cannot write.

HighOffice macro settings not enforced by policyoffice_macro_settings_weak · System Hardening5 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Microsoft Office is installed and the Office policies of the loaded user profiles (HKU, what a SYSTEM run sees) leave macros open: macros in files from the internet not blocked by policy (blockcontentexecutionfrominternet), macro antivirus scanning turned off (macroruntimescanscope = 0), the macro security level not set by policy so users can change it in the Trust Center (vbawarnings), users able to enable unsigned macros (vbawarnings not 3 or 4), or OLE packages that can run (packagerprompt not 2). Word, Excel and PowerPoint are read. One finding per setting, with how many profiles miss it. The Essential Eight levels: internet block, scanning and settings users cannot change at One, OLE packages at Two, signed or trusted macros only at Three.
Attacks it enables. Initial access through malicious documents: a phishing attachment runs macro or OLE code when the user enables content or double-clicks an embedded object.
Remediation. In the Office administrative templates (user configuration), for Word, Excel and PowerPoint: "Block macros from running in Office files from the Internet" Enabled; "VBA Macro Notification Settings" Disable all except digitally signed macros (or all macros where nobody needs them); "Macro Runtime Scan Scope" Enable for all documents; "Packager Prompt" No prompt, Object does not execute.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1566.001
ACSC Essential Eight. Restrict Microsoft Office macros, ML1: Microsoft Office macros in files originating from the internet are blocked. | Restrict Microsoft Office macros, ML1: Microsoft Office macro antivirus scanning is enabled. | Restrict Microsoft Office macros, ML1: Microsoft Office macro security settings cannot be changed by users. | Restrict Microsoft Office macros, ML3: Only Microsoft Office macros running from within a sandboxed environment, a Trusted Location or that are digitally signed by a trusted publisher are allowed to execute. | User application hardening, ML2: Microsoft Office is configured to prevent activation of Object Linking and Embedding packages.

HighUser right granted to more principals than the baselineuser_rights_excessive · Privilege Escalation5 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
Why it matters. A privilege or logon right is held by principals the CIS Level 1 baseline does not list: the Windows 11 workstation list, or on servers the member server or domain controller list of the CIS Windows Server benchmark (for example a domain controller also expects Authenticated Users and ENTERPRISE DOMAIN CONTROLLERS on network logon, and only Administrators may add workstations to the domain, which Windows grants to every authenticated user by default). Rights such as debug programs, act as part of the operating system, create a token or load drivers are a direct path to SYSTEM, so those are High; the rest are Medium. Default Windows grants some rights more widely than CIS (for example Backup Operators), so review before tightening.
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation: rights such as debug programs, backup, restore, impersonate or act as part of the OS let their holders read credentials or become SYSTEM.
Remediation. Remove the extra principals from the right (Local Security Policy > User Rights Assignment, or the matching Group Policy), after checking that no service or backup job depends on them.

HighInstalled software no longer supported by its vendorsoftware_eol · Software4 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. An installed program is a release that its vendor no longer fixes: Flash, Silverlight, Java 6/7, old Adobe Reader and Office, Exchange, SQL Server, Python, Node.js, .NET and Visual C++ runtimes past their end of life. New vulnerabilities in it stay open for good. The release is read from the Programs and Features name (and the version for Node.js) and compared with the lifecycle table in lib\Checks.ps1, or with the endoflife.date data when it was given (-EolFile or -EolOnline), which also covers Oracle Java, Eclipse Temurin, PowerShell 7, VirtualBox and Wireshark, and judges each SQL Server instance on its service pack (2016 SP2 ended years before 2016 SP3) from the build the collector reads; per-user installs are not listed. Severity follows the product: High for browser plugins, old document readers and Exchange, Low for the Visual C++ runtimes, and Low while paid extended support still runs.
Attacks it enables. Exploitation of unpatched software: the vendor no longer fixes this release, so every new vulnerability stays open for good.
Remediation. Upgrade to a supported release, or uninstall it if nothing needs it. A Visual C++ runtime may be needed by an old program: remove it with that program. For SQL Server, Extended Security Updates can cover the gap while the upgrade is planned (add an exception if the host is enrolled).

HighDomain controller weakens Netlogon or lets Server Operators schedule tasksdc_netlogon_policy_weak · Network Exposure3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The domain controller keeps an exception list for vulnerable Netlogon secure channel connections (the Zerologon, CVE-2020-1472, enforcement exceptions: the accounts listed can still use the vulnerable protocol; High), refuses machine account password changes (machine passwords never rotate; Medium), or lets Server Operators schedule tasks, which run as SYSTEM on the domain controller (Medium).

MITRE ATT&CK. T1210
CIS Windows Server 2019 v4.0.0. rule 2.3.5.1 (L1); 2.3.5.2 (L1); 2.3.5.5 (L1)
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
HighDC01Netlogon: VulnerableChannelAllowListsetnot configuredAn exception list for vulnerable Netlogon secure channel connections (Zerologon) is set (CIS Server 2019 2.3.5.2 expects it not configured).
MediumDC01Netlogon: RefusePasswordChange10RefusePasswordChange = 1: machine account passwords are never changed (CIS Server 2019 2.3.5.5 expects 0).
MediumDC01Server Operators: schedule tasks10SubmitControl = 1: Server Operators can schedule tasks that run as SYSTEM (CIS Server 2019 2.3.5.1 expects 0).
Fix script 2 of 3 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\Netlogon\Parameters'; Name = 'RefusePasswordChange'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa'; Name = 'SubmitControl'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}
# Undo: set each value back to the "old" printed above, or Remove-ItemProperty it if it was (not set).
1 finding(s) of this check have no script: follow the remediation text above.
Group Policy or Intune can set a value back: fix it there if the host is managed. Test on a pilot host first.

HighCode planted in a Windows load pointpersistence_registry_hijack · Persistence3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A registry load point starts code that is not part of Windows: an Image File Execution Options Debugger (the program named runs instead of the one started; on sethc.exe or utilman.exe it gives a SYSTEM prompt at the logon screen), a SilentProcessExit monitor, AppInit_DLLs (loaded into every program that uses user32.dll), a Winlogon Shell or Userinit other than Windows' own, or an LSA security, authentication or notification package, a print monitor or a netsh helper whose DLL is not signed by Microsoft. LSA packages see every password typed at logon. High: an unsigned LSA package, SilentProcessExit, AppInit_DLLs, a changed Winlogon value or a debugger on the logon screen tools. Medium: a vendor-signed LSA package (verify the product), an unsigned print monitor or netsh helper, another IFEO debugger. Low: a vendor-signed monitor or helper, or an entry whose file does not exist.
Attacks it enables. Persistence and credential capture: a debugger on the logon screen tools, a DLL loaded by every program, or an LSA package that sees every password typed at logon.
Remediation. Find the product the entry belongs to. If none, remove the value, delete the file it points to and investigate the host as compromised. For a legitimate vendor component, keep it current and accept it with an exception; protect LSASS (RunAsPPL) so unsigned LSA packages cannot load.

HighService program writable by usersservice_binary_writable · Privilege Escalation2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Everyone, Users, Authenticated Users or Domain Users can change the service program (High) or drop files into its folder (Medium, DLL planting; High when non-admin users can also start the service): any user gets the service account, usually SYSTEM.

HighPoint and Print allows driver installation without elevationprint_point_and_print_weak · Privilege Escalation2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The Point and Print policy lets standard users install printer drivers (RestrictDriverInstallationToAdministrators = 0) or suppresses the elevation prompt (NoWarningNoElevationOnInstall / NoWarningNoElevationOnUpdate = 1). That is the PrintNightmare condition: a standard user gets code running as SYSTEM through a driver.
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation to SYSTEM (PrintNightmare): a user installs a malicious printer driver from a server they control without an administrator prompt.
Remediation. Set RestrictDriverInstallationToAdministrators = 1 and remove the NoWarningNoElevation values (Group Policy: Point and Print Restrictions, "Show warning and elevation prompt").

HighLocal account password probably shared with other hostslocal_admin_password_shared · Credential Protection2 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
Why it matters. The same local account (the built-in Administrator by its RID 500, or the same name) had its password set at the very same second on this host and on others of the fleet: the hosts were built from one image or one script, so the password is almost certainly the same. One stolen hash then opens all of them (pass-the-hash), which is exactly what LAPS is for. Only enabled accounts are compared; domain controllers have no local accounts.
Attacks it enables. Pass-the-hash across the fleet: the hash of the shared local password, taken from one host, logs on to all the others.
Remediation. Give each host its own password: deploy Windows LAPS (it manages the built-in Administrator or a named account) and let it rotate the password now. Disable local accounts that are not needed, and deny local accounts network and Remote Desktop logon.

HighFolder of the machine PATH writable by userssystem_path_writable · Privilege Escalation2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A folder of the system PATH lets non-administrators create files in it, or does not exist and they can create it. Every process searches the PATH, services running as SYSTEM included: a DLL or program planted there runs with their rights (High for Everyone, Users, Authenticated Users or Domain Users; Medium for a single non-admin account).
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation to SYSTEM: a user drops a DLL named like one a SYSTEM service looks for and does not find (a "phantom" DLL), or a program named like a command a script calls, into the PATH folder; the service loads it at its next start.
Remediation. Remove the folder from the machine PATH if nothing needs it (an uninstalled program often leaves its entry), or fix its ACL so that only Administrators, SYSTEM and TrustedInstaller can write it. A missing folder: remove the entry, or create it with that ACL.

Fix script 2 of 2 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# SMB. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
foreach ($c in @(
    @{ Side = 'Server'; Setting = 'RequireSecuritySignature'; Value = $true }
)) {
    $cfg = if ($c.Side -eq 'Server') { Get-SmbServerConfiguration } else { Get-SmbClientConfiguration }
    'SMB {0} {1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Side, $c.Setting, $cfg.($c.Setting), $c.Value
    if ($Apply) {
        $p = @{ $c.Setting = $c.Value; Force = $true; Confirm = $false }
        if ($c.Side -eq 'Server') { Set-SmbServerConfiguration @p } else { Set-SmbClientConfiguration @p }
    }
}
# Undo: run it again with the value printed as "old".
Group Policy or Intune can set a value back: fix it there if the host is managed. Test on a pilot host first.
HighService permissions let non-administrators change or delete itservice_permissions_weak · Privilege Escalation2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The permissions of the service itself (not of its files) give an account that is not an administrator (Everyone, Users, Authenticated Users, INTERACTIVE, Domain Users, or a user outside the local Administrators group) the right to change its configuration, its permissions or its owner (High: "sc config <name> binPath= ... obj= LocalSystem" runs any program as SYSTEM), or to delete it (Medium: a user removes the service, an agent or an updater included). Starting and stopping alone are not reported: many vendors allow them on purpose; the finding names them when they come with the rights above, and the checks on writable service files take them into account.
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation: a user points the service at their own program and sets it to run as LocalSystem (sc config), then starts it. Defense evasion: a user deletes the service of an agent or updater.
Remediation. Save the current descriptor (sc.exe sdshow <name>), then remove the entry of that account or take away its change, permission, owner and delete rights (sc.exe sdset <name> <SDDL>, as an administrator). If the vendor's client needs to start its own service, keep start and stop only. Report the installer behavior to the vendor.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1543.003
SeverityHostObjectDetail
HighWKS-ACCT-017service: LegacyBackupRuns as LocalSystem (Running, start Auto): BUILTIN\Users may ChangeConfig, Start, Stop. Changing the configuration sets the program and the account it runs as: any program runs as SYSTEM.
MediumWKS-ACCT-017service: GameLauncherSvcRuns as LocalSystem (Stopped, start Auto): Everyone may Start, Stop, Delete. Deleting removes the service until it is reinstalled.

# Windows Firewall profile. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated (Group Policy can set it back).
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
foreach ($c in @(
    @{ ProfileName = 'Domain'; Setting = 'DefaultInboundAction'; Value = 'Block' }
)) {
    'Firewall {0} {1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.ProfileName, $c.Setting, (Get-NetFirewallProfile -Name $c.ProfileName).($c.Setting), $c.Value
    if ($Apply) { $p = @{ Name = $c.ProfileName; $c.Setting = $c.Value }; Set-NetFirewallProfile @p }

Fix script 1 of 1 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\LanmanWorkstation\Parameters'; Name = 'EnablePlainTextPassword'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}

Fix script 1 of 1 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# SMB. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
foreach ($c in @(
    @{ Side = 'Server'; Setting = 'EnableSMB1Protocol'; Value = $false }
)) {
    $cfg = if ($c.Side -eq 'Server') { Get-SmbServerConfiguration } else { Get-SmbClientConfiguration }
    'SMB {0} {1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Side, $c.Setting, $cfg.($c.Setting), $c.Value
    if ($Apply) {
        $p = @{ $c.Setting = $c.Value; Force = $true; Confirm = $false }
        if ($c.Side -eq 'Server') { Set-SmbServerConfiguration @p } else { Set-SmbClientConfiguration @p }
    }
}

HighNetwork logons are forced to the Guest accountforce_guest_sharing_model · Local Accounts1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. ForceGuest = 1 ("Guest only") makes every network logon to a local account run as Guest, which hides who did what and defeats per-user access control on shares.
Attacks it enables. Unaccountable access: every network logon with a local account is mapped to Guest, so anyone reaching the shares has the same rights and nothing ties actions to a person.
Remediation. Set "Network access: Sharing and security model for local accounts" to Classic - local users authenticate as themselves.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1078.001
CIS Windows 11 Enterprise v3.0.0. rule 2.3.10.12 (L1)
CIS Windows 10 Enterprise v3.0.0. rule 2.3.10.12 (L1)
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
HighWKS-ACCT-017ForceGuest1, "Guest only"0, ClassicForceGuest = 1, "Guest only" (CIS Windows 10 2.3.10.12 expects 0, Classic).
Fix script 1 of 1 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa'; Name = 'ForceGuest'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}

HighRoot certificate added outside the Microsoft trusted root programroot_ca_added · Network Exposure1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The machine's Trusted Root store has a certificate that is neither one of Windows' own nor in the Microsoft trusted root program (checked against the list of trusted roots Windows keeps, AuthRoot EncodedCtl). Whoever holds its private key can issue a certificate for any website, which every browser and program on the host trusts: TLS interception, signed malware. Games, VPNs, proxies and security tools install such roots; a root whose private key is on the host (any local administrator can export it) is High, because the same key is often on every installation. Roots pushed by Group Policy or enterprise enrollment are the organization's own choice and are not reported.
Attacks it enables. TLS interception and trusted malware: whoever holds the root's private key issues certificates the host trusts for any site or code.
Remediation. Find the program that added the root. If the organization does not need it, remove it (certlm.msc > Trusted Root Certification Authorities) and the program; if it does, push it through Group Policy so it is a documented choice, or accept it with an exception. Without Microsoft's list on the host (no automatic root update) the finding is Low: check the certificate by hand.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1553.004
SeverityHostObjectDetail
HighWKS-ACCT-017root CA: 0F1E2D3C4B5A69788796A5B4C3D2E1F00F1E2D3CFabrikam Inspection CA (Fabrikam Web Filter); valid until 2035-01-01; its private key is on this host.
HighWMI event subscription runs a command or a scriptpersistence_wmi_subscription · Persistence1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A permanent WMI event subscription (root\subscription) has a consumer that runs a command line (CommandLineEventConsumer) or a script (ActiveScriptEventConsumer). Bound to an event filter, it runs as SYSTEM whenever the event happens (a logon, a time, a process start), leaves no file in the usual autostart places and survives reboots: a favourite of fileless malware. Some management tools use them; a consumer that no binding uses is Medium.
Attacks it enables. Fileless persistence: a WMI event subscription runs the attacker's command as SYSTEM on every logon, timer or process start.
Remediation. Find out which product created the consumer. If nobody does, remove the binding, the consumer and the filter (Get-CimInstance -Namespace root/subscription ... | Remove-CimInstance) and investigate the host as compromised. Keep the WMI-Activity operational log and the ASR rule "Block persistence through WMI event subscription" on.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1546.003
SeverityHostObjectDetail
HighWKS-ACCT-017wmi consumer: UpdaterCheckCommandLineEventConsumer runs C:\Users\Public\upd.exe -q; bound to the event filter 'SELECT * FROM __InstanceModificationEvent WITHIN 60 WHERE TargetInstance ISA 'Win32_PerfFormattedData_PerfOS_System''.
HighEDR agent installed but not runningedr_agent_stopped · Antivirus1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A known endpoint detection and response (or endpoint protection) agent is installed, but its main service is not running. The host is blind to the product that is meant to protect it, and a stopped agent is also what an attacker who has disabled it leaves behind.
Attacks it enables. Blind spot: the host is not monitored, so an attacker works there without detection or response, often after stopping the agent on purpose.
Remediation. Start the service and find out why it stopped: a crash, a failed update, a policy, or someone stopping it on purpose. Check the console of the product for tamper alerts, and treat the host as unmonitored until the agent reports again.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1562.001
SeverityHostObjectDetail
HighDC01agent: CrowdStrike FalconNot running: CSFalconService (Stopped, start mode Auto).
HighLM hashes storedlm_hash_stored · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. NoLMHash is 0, so the trivially crackable LM hash is stored for local passwords set from now on.
Attacks it enables. Password cracking: LM hashes are split, uppercase and unsalted, and are cracked in seconds once an attacker dumps the SAM or NTDS.
Remediation. Set "Network security: Do not store LAN Manager hash value on next password change" to Enabled, then change the local passwords.

HighNo update installed recentlyupdates_stale · Patching1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. No update of Windows itself has been installed for longer than the threshold, so the monthly security fixes are missing. The date comes from the Windows hotfix list; the Windows Update screen can show a later date because it also lists .NET, PowerShell, Defender and app updates, which say nothing about whether Windows is patched. A host whose Windows version is out of support stops receiving these updates altogether.
Attacks it enables. Exploitation of known vulnerabilities: months of security fixes may be missing, including flaws exploited in the wild.
Remediation. Check Windows Update / WSUS / Intune for this host: failed installs, a paused ring, or a host that never reboots.

HighAutomatic logon configuredautologon_enabled · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. AutoAdminLogon = 1: Windows logs a user on at boot. When the password is stored in the registry (DefaultPassword) anyone who can read that key, and every local user can, has it in clear text; that case is High.
Attacks it enables. Credential theft: the password of the auto-logon account sits in the registry (Winlogon DefaultPassword or LSA secrets) where an administrator or malware reads it in clear text, and anyone with physical access gets a signed-in session.
Remediation. Set AutoAdminLogon to 0, delete the DefaultPassword value, and change the password that was stored there.

HighOperating system volume not encryptedbitlocker_os_volume_off · Disk and Boot1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The Windows volume is not protected by BitLocker: anyone with the disk (a stolen laptop, a copied VM disk) reads every file, cached credential and the SAM offline. High on workstations, Medium on servers.
Attacks it enables. Offline attacks on a lost or stolen device: reading files and cached credentials (SAM, LSA secrets, DPAPI keys) from the disk, or planting a backdoor by editing the system files offline.

Why it matters. A task running as SYSTEM, a service account or with highest privileges starts a program that low privileged users can change (High) or whose folder they can write (Medium).
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation and persistence: a user replaces the program a privileged scheduled task runs, and their code runs with the task's account at its next trigger.
Remediation. Fix the ACL of the program and its folder, or point the task at a protected copy.

SeverityHostObjectDetail
HighWKS-ACCT-017task: \Contoso\Inventory -> C:\Users\Public\inventory.exeRuns as SYSTEM (run level Highest): writable by Everyone on the file (FullControl).
HighLAPS not configuredlaps_not_configured · Local Accounts1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Neither Windows LAPS nor legacy Microsoft LAPS manages the local administrator password on this domain or Entra joined host, so it is likely the same on many machines.
Attacks it enables. Lateral movement with a shared password: the local administrator password is likely the same on many hosts, so one stolen hash or password opens all of them (pass-the-hash).
Remediation. Deploy Windows LAPS (BackupDirectory = Active Directory or Entra ID) through Group Policy or Intune.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1078.003
ACSC Essential Eight. Restrict administrative privileges, ML2: Credentials for break glass accounts, local administrator accounts and service accounts are long, unique, unpredictable and managed.

HighGroup Policy Preferences password (cpassword) on the hostgpp_cpassword_cached · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A Group Policy Preferences file (Groups.xml, Services.xml, ScheduledTasks.xml, DataSources.xml, Drives.xml or Printers.xml) holds a cpassword: in the copy Group Policy keeps on the host, or in SYSVOL on a domain controller. The AES key that encrypts it was published by Microsoft (MS14-025), so it is a password in clear for every user who can read the file, and in SYSVOL for every domain user. Since 2014 Windows no longer lets you create these, but old policies and their copies stay.
Attacks it enables. Credential theft from Group Policy files: the cpassword is decrypted with Microsoft's published key (Get-GPPPassword) and the account is used wherever it is valid.
Remediation. Delete the preference items that hold a password from the Group Policy objects (and the leftover files on the hosts), change every password they held, and use Windows LAPS for local administrator passwords and gMSAs for service accounts.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1552.006
SeverityHostObjectDetail
HighWKS-ACCT-017file: C:\ProgramData\Microsoft\Group Policy\History\{31B2F340-016D-11D2-945F-00C04FB984F9}\Machine\Preferences\Groups\Groups.xml1 cpassword value(s) in a Groups preference, in the copy Group Policy keeps on the host.
MediumWinRM allows Basic, Digest, unencrypted traffic, or an unsafe service configurationwinrm_insecure_auth · Network Exposure7 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The WinRM client or service policy explicitly allows Basic authentication, Digest authentication or unencrypted traffic, which exposes credentials and commands to anyone who can observe or relay the connection. The service can also store RunAs credentials for a listener (DisableRunAs = 0) or allow its configuration to be changed remotely over WinRM itself (AllowAutoConfig = 1, Level 2); disallowing remote AllowAutoConfig can break EDR/RMM tooling that configures WinRM remotely, so treat that one finding as a decision, not a default fix.
Attacks it enables. Credential capture and remote abuse: Basic or Digest authentication and unencrypted WinRM traffic expose credentials in the path, and RunAs credentials stored for plugins can be reused.
Remediation. Set the WinRM client and service policies to disallow Basic, Digest and unencrypted traffic (use Kerberos, or HTTPS listeners), and disallow storing RunAs credentials. Leave AllowAutoConfig alone if remote tooling depends on it.

Fix script 6 of 7 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\WinRM\Client'; Name = 'AllowBasic'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\WinRM\Client'; Name = 'AllowUnencryptedTraffic'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\WinRM\Client'; Name = 'AllowDigest'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\WinRM\Service'; Name = 'AllowBasic'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\WinRM\Service'; Name = 'AllowUnencryptedTraffic'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\WinRM\Service'; Name = 'DisableRunAs'; Value = 1; Type = 'DWord' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}
# Undo: set each value back to the "old" printed above, or Remove-ItemProperty it if it was (not set).

MediumDomain secure channel not protectedsecure_channel_unprotected · Network Exposure6 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The channel between this domain member and its domain controller is not required to be signed, sealed or to use a strong session key, or the machine account password is not rotated (disabled, or older than 30 days).

MediumLocal password policy weaker than the baselinepassword_policy_weak · Local Accounts6 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The local account password policy (the one that applies to local accounts; domain accounts follow the domain policy) allows short or simple passwords, never forces a change or a minimum age, remembers too few old passwords, or stores them with reversible encryption. A password that never expires is flagged here and by the per-account check.
Attacks it enables. Password guessing and cracking: short, simple or never-changing local passwords fall quickly to spraying, brute force or offline cracking of dumped hashes.
Remediation. Set history to 24, maximum age 365 days or less, minimum age 1 day or more, minimum length 14, complexity on, reversible encryption off (or manage local admin passwords with LAPS and rely on the domain policy for domain accounts).

MediumExtra member of local Administratorslocal_admin_member · Local Accounts4 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
Why it matters. An account or group other than the built-in Administrator and Domain Admins is a local administrator. Local accounts and individual users there are Medium (standing admin rights on the box, often the same password elsewhere); groups are Low (check who is inside). Attacks it enables. Privilege abuse and lateral movement: every extra administrator is another account whose theft gives full control of the host and its stored credentials.

MediumBitLocker policy weakens the protection of the disksbitlocker_policy_weak · Disk and Boot4 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A BitLocker Group Policy value lowers the protection itself: drive self-encryption trusted (several SSDs were broken, CVE-2018-12037), BitLocker allowed without a TPM, Secure Boot not used for integrity validation, no additional authentication at startup, a password protector on the OS drive. On workstations it also flags two settings whose Windows default is the problem: new DMA devices accepted while the computer is locked, and writes allowed to removable drives that are not encrypted.
Attacks it enables. Disk protection bypass: with hardware encryption allowed, no-TPM startup, DMA left open while locked or writable unprotected USB drives, a stolen device or an insider can read the data or copy it out.
Remediation. Set the listed BitLocker policies (Computer Configuration > Administrative Templates > Windows Components > BitLocker Drive Encryption) to the CIS value. Denying writes to unencrypted removable drives changes how users work with USB sticks: roll it out with BitLocker To Go. No fix script: BitLocker policy is an organizational choice.

MediumLegacy TLS/SSL protocol or weak cipher explicitly enabledschannel_legacy_crypto_enabled · Network Exposure3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The SCHANNEL registry keys switch on SSL 2.0/3.0, TLS 1.0/1.1 or a weak cipher (NULL, DES, RC2, RC4, 3DES). Only values somebody set are judged: an unset value is the Windows default. SSL and the NULL/DES/RC2/RC4 ciphers are Medium, TLS 1.0/1.1 and 3DES Low.
Attacks it enables. Traffic decryption and downgrade: an attacker in the path forces old protocols or weak ciphers (SSL 3.0, TLS 1.0, RC4) and attacks them to read or alter TLS traffic.

MediumAccount lockout missing or too lenientaccount_lockout_weak · Local Accounts3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Local accounts are never locked after failed logons (threshold 0) or after more than 5 attempts, or the lockout lasts or resets in less than 15 minutes, which leaves password guessing against local accounts practical.

MediumShares or pipes reachable without authenticationnull_session_access · Network Exposure3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Named pipes or shares are listed as accessible anonymously, or the restriction on anonymous access to pipes and shares is off (RestrictNullSessAccess = 0).

MITRE ATT&CK. T1135
CIS Windows 11 Enterprise v3.0.0. rule 2.3.10.6, 2.3.10.9, 2.3.10.11 (L1)

MediumWKS-ACCT-017NullSessionPipes1 named pipe(s) listed as accessible anonymouslynone1 named pipe(s) listed as accessible anonymously (CIS Windows 10 2.3.10.6 expects none).
MediumWKS-ACCT-017NullSessionShares2 share(s) listed as accessible anonymouslynone2 share(s) listed as accessible anonymously (CIS Windows 10 2.3.10.11 expects none).

Fix script 3 of 3 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\LanmanServer\Parameters'; Name = 'NullSessionShares'; Value = ([string[]]@()); Type = 'MultiString' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\LanmanServer\Parameters'; Name = 'NullSessionPipes'; Value = ([string[]]@()); Type = 'MultiString' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\LanmanServer\Parameters'; Name = 'RestrictNullSessAccess'; Value = 1; Type = 'DWord' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}
# Undo: set each value back to the "old" printed above, or Remove-ItemProperty it if it was (not set).
Group Policy or Intune can set a value back: fix it there if the host is managed. Test on a pilot host first.
MediumBuilt-in Administrator enabledbuiltin_admin_enabled · Local Accounts3 finding(s) on 3 host(s)
Why it matters. The RID 500 account is enabled. It cannot be locked out, and without LAPS it often shares one password across many machines (pass-the-hash everywhere). Low when LAPS manages the password.
Attacks it enables. Password attacks on a known account: the RID-500 Administrator exists on every host, is exempt from lockout by default, and its shared password lets an attacker move from host to host (pass-the-hash).
Remediation. Disable it, or let Windows LAPS manage its password.

MediumRisky service listening on the networkrisky_listener · Network Exposure3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A service often abused or unencrypted (Telnet, FTP, TFTP, SNMP, VNC, databases, caches, WinRM over HTTP on a workstation) listens on a non-loopback address.
Attacks it enables. Remote exploitation: legacy or administrative services reachable from the network (Telnet, FTP, VNC, databases, SNMP) are targets for password attacks, sniffing and exploits.

MediumScript files run when they are double-clickedscript_files_execute_on_open · System Hardening3 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A double click on a JScript, VBScript, Windows Script or HTML Application file (.js, .jse, .vbs, .vbe, .wsf, .wsh, .hta) runs it through Windows Script Host or mshta instead of opening it as text. These are the attachments and downloads that phishing uses to start malware. Read from the machine-wide association of each extension; a per-user choice is not seen. The Windows Script Host types are not judged when Windows Script Host is disabled (Settings\Enabled = 0). Medium on workstations, Low on servers.
Attacks it enables. Malware delivery by phishing: a .js, .vbs, .wsf or .hta attachment or download runs with the user's rights as soon as it is double-clicked, typically to download and start ransomware or a loader.
Remediation. Associate the script extensions with Notepad (txtfile), so a double click opens them as text; scripts still run when called explicitly (cscript, wscript, mshta, logon scripts). Use the fix script, or Group Policy (Preferences > Folder Options > Open With) for a fleet.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1204.002
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017file type: .htahtafiletxtfile (opens as text).hta opens with htafile (C:\Windows\SysWOW64\mshta.exe "%1" {1E460BD7-F1C3-4B2E-88BF-4E770A288AF5}%U{1E460BD7-F1C3-4B2E-88BF-4E770A288AF5} %*): a double click runs the script.

Fix script 3 of 3 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Classes\.js'; Name = '(default)'; Value = 'txtfile'; Type = 'String' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Classes\.vbs'; Name = '(default)'; Value = 'txtfile'; Type = 'String' }
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SOFTWARE\Classes\.hta'; Name = '(default)'; Value = 'txtfile'; Type = 'String' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}
# Undo: set each value back to the "old" printed above, or Remove-ItemProperty it if it was (not set).

MediumLocalSystem NULL session fallback or PKU2U online identities allowedlsa_weak_auth_options · Credential Protection2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. AllowNullSessionFallback or PKU2U AllowOnlineID is explicitly 1: services running as LocalSystem may fall back to anonymous NTLM sessions, or the host accepts authentication with online identities.

MediumServer does not mandate a minimum SMB dialect or SMB client encryptionsmb_server_hardening_gaps · Network Exposure2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A setting the CIS Windows Server benchmark asks for and Windows 11 does not: the SMB server has no minimum SMB 2/3 dialect set (MinSmb2Dialect), so an old client can negotiate SMB 2.0.2, or the SMB client does not require encryption (RequireEncryption), so a share without encryption is still used. Unset counts, because the Windows default enforces neither.
Attacks it enables. Downgrade and interception: older SMB dialects stay allowed, and SMB client traffic to servers that do not require encryption can be read in the path.
Remediation. Set "Mandate the minimum version of SMB" to Enabled: 3.1.1 (MinSmb2Dialect = 785) and "Require Encryption" to Enabled (RequireEncryption = 1), after checking that no legacy SMB client or server depends on the weaker setting.

MediumSaved Wi-Fi network without encryption or with WEPwifi_profile_insecure · Network Exposure2 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
Why it matters. The host keeps a Wi-Fi profile for an open network (no encryption), a WEP network or a WPA network with TKIP. If it joins on its own (connection mode auto), anyone can set up an access point with the same name nearby and the host connects to it (evil twin): its traffic and its NTLM authentication go through the attacker. Open networks joined manually and WPA with TKIP are Low. Enhanced Open (OWE) is encrypted and is not reported.
Attacks it enables. Evil twin access point: the host joins an attacker's network with the same name, which then intercepts its traffic and relays its NTLM authentication.
Remediation. Delete the profile (netsh wlan delete profile name="..."), or set it to connect manually, and push the corporate Wi-Fi with WPA2/WPA3-Enterprise through Group Policy or Intune. An always-on VPN protects the traffic on networks you cannot avoid.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1557
SeverityHostObjectDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017Wi-Fi: CafeGuest-5Gopen network, no encryption; connects automatically.
LowLT-SALES-112Wi-Fi: Hotel Lobbyopen network, no encryption; connects manually.
MediumService or task runs as a domain account with a stored passwordservice_domain_account · Credential Protection2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A service, or a scheduled task set to run whether the user is logged on or not, uses a domain user account. Windows keeps its password in the LSA secrets of this host, where any local administrator can read it in clear (secretsdump, Mimikatz lsadump::secrets). One compromised host then gives the account on every system it can reach, and such accounts are often administrators of many hosts. Group managed service accounts (name ending in $) are not reported: their password is changed by the domain and cannot be read this way.
Attacks it enables. LSA secrets dumping: a local administrator reads the domain account's password in clear (secretsdump, Mimikatz) and moves to every system the account can reach.
Remediation. Run the service as a group managed service account (gMSA), a virtual account (NT SERVICE\name) or LocalSystem/NetworkService where it only needs the computer account on the network. Where a domain account is unavoidable, give it only the rights it needs, deny it interactive logon, and do not use it on workstations.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1003.004
SeverityHostObjectDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017account: CORP\svc_backupPassword stored in the LSA secrets for 1 use(s): service BackupExecAgent (Running).
MediumWKS-ACCT-017account: CORP\svc_syncPassword stored in the LSA secrets for 1 use(s): task \Contoso\NightlySync.
MediumAudit policy gapsaudit_policy_gaps · Logging2 finding(s) on 2 host(s)
Why it matters. Subcategories the CIS benchmark expects (all 27 rules of CIS section 17: account logon and management, detailed tracking, logon/logoff, object access, policy change, privilege use, system; plus Kerberos and directory changes on DCs) are not audited, so attacks leave no event to detect.
Attacks it enables. Undetected activity: logons, privilege use, account changes or process starts that an attacker performs leave no Security log event to alert on or to investigate.
Remediation. Configure the Advanced Audit Policy for the listed subcategories (Group Policy: Advanced Audit Policy Configuration) and forward the Security log.

MediumBitLocker volume uses a weak encryption methodbitlocker_weak_encryption · Disk and Boot2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. An encrypted volume relies on the drive's own hardware encryption (self-encrypting drives: several were broken so that the data could be read without the key, CVE-2018-12037; Medium), or uses a 128-bit key or the older CBC mode instead of XTS-AES 256 (Low).
Attacks it enables. Weaker disk protection: AES-CBC 128 or hardware encryption (self-encrypting drives with known flaws) is easier to attack offline than XTS-AES 256 if the device is stolen.
Remediation. Set "Choose drive encryption method and cipher strength" to XTS-AES 256 for operating system and fixed drives and turn off hardware-based encryption, then decrypt and encrypt the volume again (the method of an encrypted volume does not change on its own).

MediumDomain controller does not require LDAP signing or channel bindingldap_server_signing_off · Network Exposure2 finding(s) on 1 host(s)

MediumWindows Server 2022 setting differs from the DISA STIGstig_server2022 · DISA STIG1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. One finding per registry rule of the DISA Microsoft Windows Server 2022 STIG (V2R8) whose value is not the required one: the domain controller rules on a domain controller, the member server rules on a member server. A value that is not configured counts, because the STIG asks for the policy to be set. Severity follows the STIG category (CAT I High, CAT II Medium, CAT III Low). Rules that need an organization decision are not judged.
Attacks it enables. Depends on the rule: each DISA STIG setting closes a specific attack path (credential theft, relay, privilege escalation, logging gaps); the rule ID in the finding names it.
Remediation. Set the policy in the finding through Group Policy, or accept the risk with an exception. The STIG rule id (V-number) in the finding identifies the rule in the DISA STIG Viewer.

MediumRestart overdue: installed updates are not activereboot_overdue · Patching1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The host has not restarted for more than 30 days, or Windows is waiting for a restart to finish installing updates (Component Based Servicing or Windows Update reboot flag) and the host has been up for more than a week. Updates to the kernel, drivers and system services take effect only after a restart, so the host stays exposed to what was patched. Over 60 days, or a pending restart, is Medium.
Attacks it enables. Exploitation of patched vulnerabilities: updates installed but not active leave the kernel and services exposed to known exploits.
Remediation. Restart the host, and set a maintenance window with Windows Update for Business, WSUS or Intune that restarts within days of the monthly updates (deadline policies).

MediumRemote UAC filtering disabled for local accountsremote_uac_token_filter_off · Local Accounts1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)

Attacks it enables. Pass-the-hash with local accounts: a local administrator's hash or password gives full remote administrative access (PsExec, WMI), the classic way to move between hosts.

MediumService path with spaces and no quotesservice_unquoted_path · Privilege Escalation1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. An unquoted service path with spaces makes Windows try "C:\Program.exe", "C:\Program Files\Some.exe" and so on first: whoever can write one of those places runs code as the service account.
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation: Windows tries each space-separated part of the unquoted path, so a user who can write to one of those folders gets a program run by the service, often as SYSTEM.
Remediation. Quote the ImagePath of the service (HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\<name>), or reinstall it with a fixed installer.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1574.009
SeverityHostObjectDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017service: ContosoUpdaterRuns as LocalSystem: C:\Program Files\Contoso Tools\Updater Service\updater.exe -service
MediumService program under a folder that a non-administrator controlsservice_path_ancestor_control · Privilege Escalation1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. A folder above the program of a service (its own folder or any folder up to the drive root, outside the Windows directory) is owned by an account that is not an administrator, or gives such an account the right to change permissions, take ownership or delete what is inside. Whoever controls a parent folder controls the whole path to the program, whatever the permissions on the program itself. High when everyone (Everyone, Users, Authenticated Users) has that control and the service runs as LocalSystem, Medium otherwise. The finding names the account and how many levels above the program the folder is; accounts that are members of the local Administrators group are not reported. The usual cause is an installer that makes the installing user the owner of the folders it creates.
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation: a user who controls a folder above the service program can rename it and put their own program in its place, which the service runs at the next start.
Remediation. As an administrator, make Administrators and SYSTEM the owners of those folders and remove the entry of the account (icacls <folder> /setowner Administrators and /remove), or move the program under Program Files. Keep a backup of the permissions first (icacls <folder> /save). Check that the vendor's updater still works afterwards, and report the installer behavior to the vendor.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1574.010
SeverityHostObjectDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017service: ContosoUpdaterRuns as LocalSystem. S-1-5-21-4000000001-4000000002-4000000003-1002 has FullControl on the folder 1 level(s) above the program's folder; S-1-5-21-4000000001-4000000002-4000000003-1002 owns the folder 2 level(s) above the program's folder. Whoever controls a parent folder controls the whole path to the program.

MediumProgram installed in a folder users can writeprogram_dir_writable · Privilege Escalation1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The install folder of a program (installed for all users) lets non-administrators create files in it, typically a program installed outside Program Files, which inherits the permissions of the drive root or ProgramData. Windows looks for a program's DLLs in its own folder first: a DLL planted there runs with the rights of whoever starts the program, an administrator included.
Attacks it enables. Privilege escalation: a user plants a DLL the program loads from its own folder (DLL search order hijacking); it runs as the next administrator who starts the program, or as SYSTEM if an installer, updater or service runs from there.
Remediation. Reinstall the program under Program Files, or fix the ACL of its folder so that only Administrators, SYSTEM and TrustedInstaller can write it. Check with the vendor before changing the ACL of a program that updates itself.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1574.001
SeverityHostObjectDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017program: Python 3.9.13 (64-bit)C:\Python39: Authenticated Users (Modify, Synchronize) may create files in the install folder. Windows looks for a program's DLLs in its own folder first: a DLL planted here runs with the rights of whoever starts the program, an administrator included. Also installed inside it: Python 3.9.13 Tcl/Tk Support. The folder is at the root of the drive, where a new folder inherits the right of Authenticated Users to change files.
MediumPowerShell history holds what looks like a passwordpowershell_history_secrets · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The PowerShell command history of a user profile (PSReadLine ConsoleHost_history.txt, kept in clear) has lines that look like they hold a secret: ConvertTo-SecureString -AsPlainText, a -Password argument with a value, net use or cmdkey with a password, a connection string with a password, or a long API key or token. HostBadger reads only how many lines match and of which kind, never the line. The file is the first thing an attacker on the host reads.
Attacks it enables. Credential theft from the command history: an attacker on the host reads ConsoleHost_history.txt and finds passwords and keys typed by administrators.
Remediation. Open the history of that profile, change every password or key it holds, and clear the lines (or the file). Pass credentials with Get-Credential, a secret vault or a managed identity, not on the command line; PSReadLine can be told not to save sensitive lines (Set-PSReadLineOption -AddToHistoryHandler).
MITRE ATT&CK. T1552.001
SeverityHostObjectDetail

Attacks it enables. Password hash cracking: LM and NTLMv1 responses captured on the network (Responder) are cracked quickly or turned into the account's NTLM hash.
Remediation. Set "Network security: LAN Manager authentication level" to "Send NTLMv2 response only. Refuse LM & NTLM" (5) after auditing NTLMv1 use.

Fix script 1 of 1 finding(s) · read it, run it elevated in Windows PowerShell 5.1; it changes nothing until $Apply = $true 
# Registry values. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated.
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
$changes = @(
    @{ Path = 'HKLM:\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa'; Name = 'EveryoneIncludesAnonymous'; Value = 0; Type = 'DWord' }
)
foreach ($c in $changes) {
    $key = Get-Item -LiteralPath $c.Path -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue
    $vn = if ($c.Name -eq '(default)') { '' } else { $c.Name }   # the default value is '' to the registry API
    $old = if ($key -and ($key.GetValueNames() -contains $vn)) { $key.GetValue($vn) } else { '(not set)' }
    '{0}\{1}: {2} -> {3}' -f $c.Path, $c.Name, $old, $(if ($c.Type -eq 'MultiString') { '(empty)' } else { $c.Value })
    if ($Apply) {
        if (-not $key) { New-Item -Path $c.Path -Force | Out-Null }
        Set-ItemProperty -LiteralPath $c.Path -Name $c.Name -Value $c.Value -Type $c.Type
    }
}

MediumLDAP client does not require encryptionldap_client_encryption_weak · Network Exposure1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Network security: LDAP client encryption requirements (LDAPClientConfidentiality) is below Negotiate sealing (1), so the server can make LDAP binds that are not encrypted and a man in the middle can read or tamper with the directory traffic. Unset counts as None (0).
Attacks it enables. Interception: LDAP traffic to domain controllers can travel without encryption, exposing directory data and credentials to an attacker in the path.
Remediation. Set "Network security: LDAP client encryption requirements" to Negotiate sealing or Require sealing (LDAPClientConfidentiality = 1 or 2) through Group Policy, after confirming the directory clients support it.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1557
CIS Windows Server 2022 v4.0.0. rule 2.3.11.8 (L1)
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
MediumDC01LDAP client encryption01 or 2 (Negotiate/Require sealing)LDAPClientConfidentiality = 0 (CIS Server 2022 2.3.11.8 expects at least 1, Negotiate sealing).
MediumKerberos allows RC4 or DESkerberos_rc4_allowed · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Network security: Configure encryption types allowed for Kerberos (SupportedEncryptionTypes) is not set to AES only, or is not set, which leaves RC4 (and on old builds DES) in use. Kerberos tickets encrypted with RC4 use the NT hash as the key, so Kerberoasting and AS-REP roasting crack them quickly, and an NT hash can be passed as a Kerberos key (overpass-the-hash). DES is High.
Attacks it enables. Kerberoasting and overpass-the-hash: RC4 tickets are cracked offline quickly, and an NT hash is usable as a Kerberos key.
Remediation. Set "Network security: Configure encryption types allowed for Kerberos" to AES128_HMAC_SHA1, AES256_HMAC_SHA1 and Future encryption types (SupportedEncryptionTypes = 2147483640) through Group Policy, after checking that no service account or appliance still needs RC4 (msDS-SupportedEncryptionTypes on the accounts, and the Kerberos events 4768/4769 with ticket encryption type 0x17).
MITRE ATT&CK. T1558.003
CIS Windows 11 Enterprise v3.0.0. rule 2.3.11.4 (L1)
CIS Windows 10 Enterprise v3.0.0. rule 2.3.11.4 (L1)
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017SupportedEncryptionTypesnot set2147483640SupportedEncryptionTypes not set (CIS Windows 10 2.3.11.4 expects 2147483640).
MediumInternet Explorer 11 is enabledie11_enabled · System Hardening1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The Internet Explorer 11 optional feature is enabled and no policy turns it off as a standalone browser (NotifyDisableIEOptions = 1). IE11 is out of support and is the last browser that ran Java applets and ActiveX from the internet. Windows 11 does not have it; Windows 10 LTSC and Windows Server still can. An Essential Eight Maturity Level One requirement (it also covers "web browsers do not process Java from the internet").
Attacks it enables. Drive-by compromise through an unsupported browser engine: exploits for IE11, ActiveX and Java applets that modern browsers no longer run.
Remediation. Turn off the feature (Disable-WindowsOptionalFeature -Online -FeatureName Internet-Explorer-Optional-amd64) or set "Disable Internet Explorer 11 as a standalone browser" to Always; sites that need it go to Edge IE mode.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1189
ACSC Essential Eight. User application hardening, ML1: Internet Explorer 11 is disabled or removed. | User application hardening, ML1: Web browsers do not process Java from the internet.
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017Internet Explorer 11enableddisabled or removedThe Internet-Explorer-Optional-amd64 feature is enabled and NotifyDisableIEOptions is not 1.
MediumMark of the Web is not applied to files copied from insecure sourcesexplorer_motw_bypass · System Hardening1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. The "Do not apply the Mark of the Web tag to files copied from insecure sources" policy is Enabled (DisableMotWOnInsecurePathCopy = 1), so files copied from a network share or other insecure location keep no zone marker. SmartScreen, Office Protected View and macro blocking all rely on that marker, so the setting removes a key defence against downloaded malware.
Attacks it enables. Phishing payload execution: files copied from insecure locations lose the Mark of the Web, so SmartScreen, Office Protected View and macro blocking do not apply to them.
Remediation. Set "Do not apply the Mark of the Web tag to files copied from insecure sources" to Disabled (DisableMotWOnInsecurePathCopy = 0) through Group Policy (Windows Components > File Explorer).
MITRE ATT&CK. T1553.005
CIS Windows Server 2019 v4.0.0. rule 18.10.29.2 (L1)
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
MediumDC01Explorer: Mark of the Web on insecure copy10DisableMotWOnInsecurePathCopy = 1: files copied from insecure sources keep no zone marker (CIS Server 2019 18.10.29.2 expects 0, the policy Disabled).
MediumMicrosoft vulnerable driver blocklist turned offdriver_blocklist_off · Privilege Escalation1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. VulnerableDriverBlocklistEnable is set to 0: Windows loads drivers that Microsoft has blocked because attackers abuse them to reach the kernel. It is on by default on Windows 11 22H2 and later and with HVCI; a value that is not set is not judged.
Attacks it enables. Kernel compromise (BYOVD): an attacker with administrator rights loads a signed but vulnerable driver to kill EDR, read credentials or run code in the kernel.
Remediation. Turn on Windows Security > Device security > Core isolation > Microsoft Vulnerable Driver Blocklist (VulnerableDriverBlocklistEnable = 1, restart). If a business driver is blocked, update it instead of turning the list off.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1068
ACSC Essential Eight. Application control, ML3: Microsoft's vulnerable driver blocklist is implemented.

Why it matters. Without tamper protection, a local administrator (or malware running as one) can disable Defender, add exclusions or stop real-time protection.

Why it matters. The antivirus signatures were last updated more days ago than the threshold, so recent malware is not recognized.
Attacks it enables. Known malware is missed: threats released since the last signature update are not recognized.

MediumDefender Network Protection not enableddefender_network_protection_off · Antivirus1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Network Protection is off or only auditing, so connections to known malicious sites and servers are not blocked at the network layer.
Attacks it enables. Malicious connections are not blocked: users and malware reach phishing pages, exploit sites and command-and-control servers that Defender SmartScreen reputation would stop.

# Microsoft Defender. Windows PowerShell 5.1, elevated (tamper protection or a policy can refuse the change).
$Apply = $false   # set to $true to apply; with $false it only shows what would change
foreach ($c in @(
    @{ Setting = 'EnableNetworkProtection'; Value = 1 }
)) {
    'Defender {0}: {1} -> {2}' -f $c.Setting, (Get-MpPreference).($c.Setting), $c.Value
    if ($Apply) { $p = @{ $c.Setting = $c.Value }; Set-MpPreference @p }
}

MediumCredential Guard not runningcredential_guard_off · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Credential Guard keeps NTLM hashes and Kerberos tickets in an isolated process that even SYSTEM cannot read. Without it, a single local admin compromise yields reusable domain credentials.
Attacks it enables. Credential theft from memory: NTLM hashes and Kerberos tickets in LSASS can be dumped (mimikatz) and reused for pass-the-hash and pass-the-ticket.
Remediation. Enable Virtualization-based Security with Credential Guard (Group Policy: Device Guard > Turn On Virtualization Based Security) on hardware that supports it. Not supported on domain controllers.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1003.001
ACSC Essential Eight. Restrict administrative privileges, ML3: Credential Guard functionality is enabled.
SeverityHostObjectDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017Credential GuardNot running (VBS status 0: 0 off, 1 configured, 2 running).
MediumCommand lines missing from process eventscmdline_audit_off · Logging1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Event 4688 does not record the command line, which is what tells a normal PowerShell or cmd.exe start from a malicious one.
Attacks it enables. Undetected execution: process start events (4688) carry no command line, so encoded PowerShell, LOLBins and attacker tools run without the arguments that reveal them.
Remediation. Enable "Include command line in process creation events" with Audit Process Creation (Success).
MITRE ATT&CK. T1562.002
ACSC Essential Eight. User application hardening, ML2: Command line process creation events are centrally logged.

MediumBrowser extension installed outside the storebrowser_extension_sideloaded · Software1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. An enabled browser extension did not come from the Chrome Web Store, the Edge Add-ons store or addons.mozilla.org, and was not pushed by the organization's policy: it is loaded from a folder in developer mode, installed from a .crx file, installed by another program, or (Firefox) not signed by Mozilla. Nobody reviewed it, it does not update from the store, and this is how adware and info-stealers add their extension. One finding per extension, with the browsers and profiles that have it.
Attacks it enables. Browser persistence: malware or adware adds its own extension (developer mode, a .crx file or an external install) to spy on browsing and steal session cookies, past the store review.
Remediation. Find out who installed it and why; remove it if nobody needs it. Turn developer mode off by policy (DeveloperToolsAvailability / ExtensionDeveloperModeSettings), allow only store or policy installs (ExtensionInstallSources, ExtensionAllowedTypes) and push the extensions the organization needs with ExtensionInstallForcelist.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1176
SeverityHostObjectDetail

MediumBlank passwords allowed outside the consoleblank_password_network_logon · Local Accounts1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. LimitBlankPasswordUse = 0 lets local accounts with an empty password log on over the network (SMB, RDP, remote tools), not just at the keyboard.
Attacks it enables. Remote logon without a password: an attacker on the network signs in over SMB, RDP or WinRM with any local account whose password is empty.
Remediation. Set "Accounts: Limit local account use of blank passwords to console logon only" to Enabled and give every local account a password.

MediumStandard ASR rules not in block modeasr_standard_rules_missing · Antivirus1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Microsoft recommends three attack surface reduction rules for every device (block credential stealing from LSASS, abuse of vulnerable signed drivers, persistence through WMI event subscriptions). They are not all set to block.
Attacks it enables. Credential theft and persistence stay open: reading LSASS memory (mimikatz), loading vulnerable signed drivers, and WMI event subscription persistence are not blocked.

MITRE ATT&CK. T1003.001

MediumNo application control enforcedapp_control_not_enforced · System Hardening1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. Neither App Control for Business (WDAC) enforces a user-mode policy (Win32_DeviceGuard UsermodeCodeIntegrityPolicyEnforcementStatus = 2) nor AppLocker enforces executable rules (rules in the Exe collection, not in audit-only mode, Application Identity service not disabled): any program a user downloads or brings runs. A policy in audit mode is named in the detail. Smart App Control blocks by reputation, not against a list the organization approved: it lowers the severity to Low but does not count. The Essential Eight wants application control on workstations from Maturity Level One (internet-facing servers at Two, all servers at Three).
Attacks it enables. Execution of anything a user downloads or brings: a phishing payload, a dropped tool, a portable remote access program or ransomware runs straight from Downloads or Temp.
Remediation. Deploy App Control for Business (WDAC) in enforced mode, starting from a Microsoft base policy with Microsoft's recommended block rules, after an audit period; or AppLocker with rules for executables, scripts, installers and DLLs, and the Application Identity service set to start. Both are organizational choices that need testing: there is no fix script.
MITRE ATT&CK. T1204.002
ACSC Essential Eight. Application control, ML1: Application control is implemented on workstations. | Application control, ML3: Application control is implemented on non-internet-facing servers.
SeverityHostObjectFoundRequiredDetail
MediumWKS-ACCT-017Application controlWDAC user mode: off; AppLocker executables: audit onlyWDAC user-mode policy or AppLocker executable rules, enforcedWDAC user mode: off; AppLocker executables: audit only.

Why it matters. NoAutoUpdate = 1: the Automatic Updates policy is set to Disabled, so security updates are not installed unless someone does it by hand.
Attacks it enables. Exploitation of known vulnerabilities: security updates are not installed on their own, so the host falls behind on fixes.
Remediation. Set "Configure Automatic Updates" to Enabled (or Not configured on managed rings) and check that WSUS / Intune delivers the updates.

MediumLSASS not running as a protected processlsa_not_protected · Credential Protection1 finding(s) on 1 host(s)
Why it matters. RunAsPPL is not set, so any process with debug rights can open LSASS and read the credentials cached in it (Mimikatz-style dumping).
Attacks it enables. Credential dumping: without LSA protection, tools such as mimikatz read LSASS memory to collect password hashes, Kerberos tickets and sometimes clear-text passwords.
Remediation. Set HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Lsa RunAsPPL = 1 (or 2 for UEFI lock) through Group Policy or Intune, after checking that no LSA plug-in or driver is incompatible.
